An opposition candidate would face new bureaucratic interests, especially in the transportation\nand logistics sectors, which may be viewed increasingly as the military’s purview. More recently, AMLO’s\ndecision to order SEMAR to conduct a “temporary occupation” of a privately operated rail line along the\nIsthmus of Tehuantepec should be cause for even greater concern, suggesting a role for the military not merely\nin operating public infrastructure projects but also in the seizure and administration of heretofore private\nfacilities.58 If such moves become more commonplace, the militarization of Mexico’s economy will only further\nweaken investor confidence and depress economic growth.\nSignificant questions remain as to how entrenched these interests will be by 2024 and what clashes may occur\nwithin the army and navy on matters of professional ethics and the role of the armed forces. If a successor\nfrom Morena helms the 2024 government and continues along the same course, they may empower an armed\nforces constituency for years to come. As such, the legacy of institutional decline and alteration will persist\nlong after AMLO departs.\n12 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer", "excerpt_sha256": "237af2342d10c22e840ab3122844ff7d6a74de5aad5b843d548a4ff49d50c55f", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E019", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "CSIS reported budget cuts and political pressure affecting the INE, INAI, COFECE and CNDH; alleged attacks on NGOs, defenders, media and anti-corruption initiatives; and an electoral Plan B that reduced the electoral arbiter’s powers and was fast-tracked. The Supreme Court later invalidated important parts of Plan B, suspended other provisions and preserved institutional checks, while the INE conducted successful local elections in June 2023.", "question_ids": ["4B.1", "4B.10", "4B.2", "4B.3", "4B.4", "4B.6", "4B.9", "8B.2", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly relevant to 2023, including the Senate’s February approval and court decisions through June 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The reforms, budget proposals and attacks were attributed to López Obrador; the constraints came from Congress, courts and civil society.", "limitations": ["The report uses strong analytical language and incorporates cited commentary from other observers."], "start_unit": 26, "end_unit": 28, "locator": "page 26 through page 28", "exact_excerpt": "Politics\nThe election of López Obrador in 2018 revealed a country whose citizens are highly polarized and\ndominated by strong feelings of disillusionment with the political establishment. Building an anti-\ncorruption, anti-neoliberal economic discourse while avoiding firm positions on controversial issues\nsuch as gay marriage or abortion rights, AMLO managed to appeal to voters across a wide range of political\norientations.110 Once in power, however, he used the excuse of combating corruption to justify his need to\nfully command the government and wield more power than his predecessors.111 Who will occupy the Palacio\nNacional in 2024 remains an open question, but regardless of the outcome, AMLO’s attacks on democratic\ninstitutions, the dynamics of the Morena party and the opposition, and U.S.-Mexico relations will heavily shape\nboth the campaign and post-campaign environments.\nAttacks against Democracy\nAMLO’s style has steadily become more populist, and his attacks against independent democratic institutions built\nover the past 30 years have led Mexico down a perilous route. Especially concerning has been his weaponization of\nanti-corruption rhetoric in Mexico, the crutch that supports many of his most controversial political projects.\nHis strategy to silence critics and avoid checks and balances has broken institutions, cutting their budgets and\nplacing loyalists in key positions of power. The INE, the Federal Economic Competition Commission (COFECE),\nand the INAI have all seen their budgets slashed.112 The proposal for the 2023 budget published in November 2022\nfit this trend. It cut the INE’s budget by nearly 4.5 billion pesos, the INAI’s by 18.6 million, and the COFECE’s by\n33.5 million.113 In the case of the National Human Rights Commission (CNDH), the controversial election of Rosario\nPiedra Ibarra, who openly supported AMLO’s political projects, as its head raised concerns over the extent to which\nthe commission could remain silent over human rights violations committed by the military and simultaneously\n22 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\noperate independently from the government.114 Piedra spoke in favor of AMLO’s electoral reform, explicitly showing\nhow the commission is politically aligned with AMLO and how its autonomy has been compromised.115\nAlong the same lines, AMLO has denied human rights abuses and has directly attacked nongovernmental\norganizations, human rights defenders, women’s organizations, civil society movements, the media, and genuine\nanti-corruption initiatives.116 Some of his actions have undermined the government’s division of powers, verging\non illegality and unconstitutional action. These include his appointment of finance ministers before going through\nthe appropriate legislative procedure, weaponization of the judicial branch by threatening professors and scientists\nwith incarceration, organizing unlawful press conferences in support of his political party during the referendum\ncampaign, and using tax authority and never-ending audits to suppress corruption investigations.117\nOne of the most recent examples of blatant democratic backsliding is the electoral system reform AMLO\ncontinued to push for in an effort return to a time when the party in power controlled the electoral process.118\nAfter he abandoned his initial reform proposal due to lack of consensus in the Chamber of Deputies, AMLO\ndevised Plan B, which required only a simple majority for approval. This second proposal still reduced the\nsize of the organization, removed powers from the electoral arbiter limiting its ability to enforce electoral\nlaws, and softened sanctions for political parties. The plan was fast-tracked for approval in November 2022,\nundermining thorough analysis and discussion and circumventing the opposition almost entirely.119 The speed\nand coordination with which the reform came into being led many to conclude it had been AMLO’s plan all\nalong. On February 22, 2023, the plenary of the Senate approved the electoral reform without an eternal life\nclause, an article that allows the transfer of votes between parties forming a coalition in order to prevent\nthe disappearance of parties with little electoral strength, which struggled to find consensus.120 Indeed, the\nelectoral reform seems to have come as the most brazen of AMLO’s attacks against democracy, perhaps in\nan attempt to make the political context more favorable for the election of his preferred successor in 2024.\nThis has, in turn, provoked a forceful reaction among Mexican civil society, as there is unanimity among\nacademics, businesspeople, and political analysts that Mexican democracy rarely has sunk so low.121\nNot only did the electoral reform engender massive protests, but it also helped the opposition fully unify for\nthe first time since the beginning of AMLO’s mandate, bringing his approval rating to the lowest level since he\ncame to office.122 Popular pushback has been accompanied by resistance by some institutions remaining faithful\nto democratic principles. First among these is the Supreme Court. In April 2022 AMLO organized a referendum\non the president’s adequacy to remain in charge until the end of his mandate.123 Surprisingly, the court ruled\nin favor of the referendum regardless of the extremely low budget AMLO allocated to conducting it adequately.\nNevertheless, the Supreme Court demonstrated independence and eventually deemed the referendum invalid,\ndeclaring it undermined democracy by directly affecting citizens’ participation in the political process.124\nFurthermore, the opposition welcomed the appointment of Chief Justice Norma Lucía Piña Hernández in early\nJanuary 2023.125 As she did not belong to AMLO’s entourage, her election came as a positive sign for Mexican\ndemocracy. She is the first female chief justice and a supporter of the court’s independence and of human rights,\nespecially women’s rights.126 Together with other justices, many of whom have been criticized for siding with\nAMLO, she will review numerous pieces of legislation challenged by the opposition currently sitting before the\ncourt, including the decree extending the militarization of public security.127 Justice Alberto Pérez Dayán suspended\nthe first part of AMLO’s Plan B from entering into effect in the states where the electoral process had already\nbegun (Coahuila and State of Mexico). The court’s decision was subsequently validated as the INE organized two\nremarkably successful and transparent elections on June 4, demonstrating its continued trustworthiness and\nrelevance.128 The INE won further victories. In late June, the Supreme Court invalidated important parts of Plan B.\nIn response, AMLO promised to double down on Plan C, and promised to continue with his proposed constitutional\n23 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nreform that would allow the general public to elect judges and magistrates, in light of his belief that the judiciary\nrepresents opposition and conservative interests.\nMost recently, AMLO ordered senators to hold a session in a separate chamber to exclude the opposition. At\nthis unconstitutional session, the Senate passed 20 reforms in five hours on all manner of topics, including\nnew mining regulations that effectively shut down the industry in Mexico, ceding control and a majority of\nrevenues from the Maya Train and airports to the Mexican army. Senators also established a new tourism\nagency, 80 percent of whose fees would be allocated to the army. Many of the reforms, which included several\nconstitutional changes, run afoul of the USMCA. Such blatant violation of democratic procedure was evident in\npictures from the scene, which featured senators voting in the middle of the night on legislation they had not\nread, seated at plastic picnic tables without microphones.129\nTHE DEMOCRACY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWith the end of term of the INE’s 11 commissioners in 2023, AMLO will likely want to appoint more members\nloyal to him to further reduce the independence of Mexico’s electoral system.130 Some analysts fear that if the\nappointment is brought to a successful conclusion, together with the reform of the electoral process, Mexico’s\ndemocratic institutions will be further weakened, and the nation might not have free and fair elections in 2024.\nAMLO is dedicated to ensuring his preferred candidate is elected and continues his political agenda. In fact, he\nhas even entertained ideas of breaking the cardinal rule in Mexican politics and standing for a second term.131\nResistance by the opposition and the population plays a pivotal role in checking AMLO’s extension of power\nand his impact on democratic institutions. An even more crucial position is that of the Supreme Court, called\nto officially demonstrate its independence by voting on the constitutionality of Plan B, which is expected to\ntake place by June. This will continue to be key, especially as the 2024 electoral campaign gets closer and the\nMexican social, political, and economic context becomes more fragile.\nFigure 8: Mexico’s Democracy Index Score over Time\n8\nFlawed Democracy\n7\n6\n5\nHybrid Regime\n4\n2006 2008 2010 2012 2014 2016 2018 2020 2022\nSource: Economist Intelligence Unit (EIU), Democracy Index 2022 (EIU, 2022), https://www.eiu.com/n/campaigns/democracy-index-\n2022/#:~:text=Indicators,political%20participation%20and%20political%20culture.\nOn May 18, 2023, the Supreme Court denied AMLO’s claim that the permitting process for pet projects like the\nMaya Train could be expedited because they represented matters of “national security.” Mere hours after the\nverdict was handed down, a near-identical order was announced, redesignating the Maya Train, along with\n24 | Ryan C. A lo largo de la actual admi-\n                 nistración, algunos programas han sido eliminados de la lista y otros más se han incluido,\n                 estos cambios dan como resultado la existencia de 16 programas prioritarios de desarrollo\n                 social para el año 2022. La operación de estos se encuentra a cargo de seis dependencias\n                 federales, la Secretaría de Bienestar es la que mayor número de programas tiene a su cargo\n                 (seis programas).\n\n\n          •      En 2022, el presupuesto original de los programas prioritarios es de $506,543 millones de\n                 pesos (que representa el 40 % del presupuesto total asignado a todos los programas y accio-\n                 nes sociales), lo cual equivale a un incremento, en términos reales, del 71 %, respecto al pre-\n                 supuesto asignado en 2019. El Programa Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas\n                 Mayores (S176) obtuvo el mayor presupuesto original con $238,014.73 millones de pesos y el\n                 Programa para el Bienestar de las Personas en Emergencia Social o Natural (U011) obtuvo\n                 el menor presupuesto original con $510.59 millones de pesos, ambos son coordinados por\n                 la Secretaría de Bienestar.\n\n\n          •      El Diagnóstico de Matrices 20221 reveló que, en general, se observa una mejoría en cuanto\n                 al diseño de la MIR de los programas prioritarios. En 2022, el 50 % obtuvo una calificación de\n                 diseño adecuada o destacada, esto representa un aumento de 24 puntos porcentuales en\n                 comparación con 2020. Como consecuencia, se observa también una disminución signifi-\n                 cativa de los programas que tienen matrices con oportunidad de mejora; en 2020 había\n                 ocho programas en este rango y para 2022 solo dos mantienen este nivel.\n\n\n          •      De los 16 programas prioritarios de desarrollo social, el 75% lograron identificar de manera\n                 total o parcial la temática que atienden. En el periodo 2019-2022, ocho registraron cambios\n                 en sus temáticas de atención: tres de los cuales fueron producto de un cambio de enfoque\n                 de los programas; tres más pasaron de no identificar una temática a poder identificarla de\n                 manera clara y los dos restantes perdieron claridad en la identificación de este aspecto.\n\n\n\n1\n    El Diagnóstico de Matrices 2022 puede consultarse en la siguiente liga: https://www.coneval.org.mx/coordinacion/IMPPS/Paginas/Diagnosticos-de-Monitoreo-de-progra-\n    mas-y-politicas-sociales.aspx\n\n\n\n\n                                       8              Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2022: estado actual al cuarto año de la Administración y resultados alcanzados en 2021\n\n•      A través del análisis de poblaciones objetivo, los programas prioritarios se clasificaron en\n                  seis grupos: jóvenes, niñas y niños, productores, población en condiciones de vulnerabilidad,\n                  personas adultas mayores y personas con discapacidad. No obstante, aún hay cuatro que\n                  no tienen claridad respecto a la población objetivo que buscan atender, a este grupo se les\n                  asignó la categoría de “población objetivo no identificada”.\n\n\n           •      Al analizar la concentración presupuestal por grupo poblacional, se observa que las perso-\n                  nas adultas mayores tienen la mayor parte del presupuesto de los programas prioritarios con\n                  el 47 %. En contraste, la población en condiciones de vulnerabilidad2 es el grupo con menor\n                  presupuesto ya que únicamente tiene el 1 %.\n\n\n           •      La valoración del Enfoque de Resultados (EdR) de los 16 programas prioritarios mostró que\n                  el 63 % (10) de estos lograron identificar, de manera conjunta, tanto su problemática pública\n                  como su población objetivo. De este conjunto, seis que en 2019 no identificaban estos ele-\n                  mentos o lo hacían de manera parcial, lograron identificarlos plenamente en 2022; no obs-\n                  tante, tres programas que identificaban su problema público en 2019 perdieron precisión en\n                  su identificación en 2022.\n\n\n           •      Los principales retos del EdR se encuentran en la identificación de la población objetivo y la\n                  medición de resultados. En la primera, hubo un retroceso respecto a 2019, ya que 14 de los\n                  16 programas prioritarios identificaban de manera clara a su población, mientras que, en\n                  2022, solo 12 programas la identifican. En cuanto a la medición de resultados se encontró\n                  que el 31 % de los programas aún no cuentan con indicadores apropiados para monitorear\n                  el logro de sus objetivos centrales.\n\n\n           •      El estudio de cobertura reveló que de forma general, los programas prioritarios, atendieron\n                  en 2021 a 27 millones de personas mexicanas, cifra equivalente al 64 % de la población\n                  objetivo de los programas. De 2020 a 2021, los programas que incrementaron en mayor me-\n                  dida su población atendida son Pensión para el Bienestar de las Personas Adultas Mayores\n                  (S176), Programa de Becas de Educación Básica para el Bienestar Benito Juárez (S072) y\n                  Beca Universal para Estudiantes de Educación Media Superior Benito Juárez (S311). Entre los\n                  tres programas incorporaron a 5.6 millones de personas beneficiarias, es decir, un incremen-\n                  to del 26 % respecto del año previo. De manera coincidente, estos programas son los que\n                  tuvieron el porcentaje de cobertura más alto, por encima del 70 % en 2021.\n\n\n           •      Los resultados del Cumplimiento Efectivo de Metas demuestran que el 56 % de los progra-\n                  mas prioritarios tuvieron un cumplimiento adecuado o destacado en 2021. Al comparar los\n                  resultados 2020 y 2021, se observa que cuatro programas que contaban con un nivel bajo\n                  o deficiente en 2020 lograron ubicarse como adecuados o destacados en 2021, en este\n                  grupo se encuentran los dos programas prioritarios coordinados por la Sedatu.\n2\n    La población objetivo en condiciones de vulnerabilidad para efectos del presente análisis, es aquella que por su entorno socioeconómico presenta alguna condición que\n    vulnere su acceso efectivo a algún derecho social (artículo 6to de la Ley General de Desarrollo Social (LGDS), y que no se encuentra dentro de las siguientes poblaciones:\n    adultos mayores, jóvenes, personas con discapacidad y/o productores\n\n\n\n\n                                                              Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   9", "excerpt_sha256": "8bd42e41dd6ca77fb21dc0343ec4db799903fe16eb89bd70ac562a8dd8ee8c86", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0052-E002", "source_id": "SRC-9a96a4fbd3b1a6f5", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Informe_Programas_Prioritarios_2023.pdf", "title": "Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "fca21ecce6ebb306d276d4e77de72438ea1fc2a4682148a26f6a8377e710c60f", "fact_summary": "CONEVAL found that 13 of 16 priority programs adequately identified their target population in 2023, but three did not. Cabe precisar que, para definir y delimitar la po-\n     blación objetivo, el Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social (CONEVAL)\n     siempre recalca la importancia de que los programas se basen en la Metodología de Marco Lógico\n     (MML) para que estos midan resultados en la población que atienden y, de esta manera, se fortalezca\n     la rendición de cuentas.\n\n\n     A continuación, la figura 6 describe los objetivos de Propósito de los tres programas que presentan\n     áreas de oportunidad en la identificación de su población objetivo.\n\n\n     Figura 6. Objetivo de Propósito de los programas U083, S281, U013, 2023\n\n\n\n                                                   U083 - Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (SEP)\n\n\n              Superar la exclusión educativa de tipo superior contribuye a disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios.\n\n\n\n                                                           S281 - Programa Nacional de Reconstrucción (Sedatu)\n\n\n             Los municipios afectados por los sismos de septiembre de 2017 y febrero de 2018 recuperan los inmuebles de salud,\n                               educación, vivienda y bienes culturales dañados, para beneficio de la población.\n\n\n                    U013 - Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (Salud)\n\n\n\n                                                  Se cuenta con transferencia de recursos a las Entidades Federativas.\n\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n     Como se aprecia, los programas U013 y U083 no especifican ningún grupo poblacional al cual bus-\n     can atender. El U013 tampoco precisa cuál es el resultado o cambio que se propone, dado que su\n     objetivo se limita a la entrega de un bien (transferencia), lo cual no es un resultado sino un tema de\n     Gestión. En lugar de identificar un problema público a solucionar y la población afectada por este\n     problema, solo se distribuye un bien, lo que es distintivo de un Componente de un programa y no del\n\n\n\n19   Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023\n     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social\n\nobjetivo central, del motor y eje de un programa. Por su parte, el objetivo que plantea el programa\n     U083 es más apropiado para un objetivo de Fin, pues menciona el resultado al que busca contribuir\n     (“disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios”).\n\n\n     Es completa responsabilidad del programa resolver la problemática central que se plantea como Pro-\n     pósito. La exclusión educativa de tipo superior es un objetivo muy amplio, por lo resulta confuso qué\n     es lo que el programa persigue. Los derechos sociales a la educación y a la salud son fundamentales\n     para el bienestar de la población mexicana; por lo tanto, como dichos programas son indispensables\n     para hacer efectiva su garantía, es importante que el diseño del Propósito de los tres programas se\n     apegue a la MML.\n\n\n     Por su parte, el programa S281 señala a los “municipios afectados por los sismos”, sin especificar el\n     tipo de afectación ni las características que deben cumplir los municipios para acceder a los apoyos.\n     Cabe recordar que los programas sociales se crean para resolver problemas que afectan a personas,\n     los cuales, en el caso de los programas prioritarios, son de carácter urgente pues son graves o afectan\n     a poblaciones vulnerables. Si bien el daño a la infraestructura de salud, educación, vivienda y bienes\n     culturales de los municipios afectados por los sismos es, en efecto, un problema grave, quien sufre es-\n     tos daños es la población que habita en esos municipios, la cual presenta características particulares,\n     ya sea jóvenes o adultos mayores, personas indígenas o afromexicanas, que habiten en localidades\n     con alto rezago social o cuyos ingresos sean insuficientes para acceder a servicios privados de las\n     vertientes que el programa atiende. Por ello, es clave que este programa identifique adecuadamente\n     cuál es su población objetivo.\n\n\n     Como ejemplo de lo anterior, la figura 7 muestra el Propósito del S273 “Programa de Mejoramiento\n     Urbano (PMU)”, coordinado igualmente por Sedatu. En su objetivo central, tanto la población que\n     atiende como el resultado que espera están claramente identificados. Al delimitar su población ob-\n     jetivo considerando elementos como el rezago urbano y social, se comprende cuál es el problema\n     que la aqueja y su dimensión. Así, el programa cuenta con elementos para reportar sus resultados de\n     manera oportuna.\n\n\n     Figura 7. SUMMARY OF PROGRAM IMPLEMENTATION\nProgram and Portfolio Performance\n13. World Bank lending to Mexico has increased, driven by high growth in policy lending. IBRD lending\nhas increased by more than 25 percent in this CPF period relative to the previous one (FY14-19), propelled\nby an eightfold increase in DPF net commitments. At the end of FY23, total approved lending during the CPF\nperiod was US$4.3 billion (US$3.9 billion in DPF), versus US$3.4 billion during the previous CPF (US$0.5 billion\nin DPF). One additional DPF is planned for FY24. IFC’s commitments increased from US$1.62 billion in FY14-\n17 to US$1.75 billion in FY20-23. Annex 5 provides an overview of portfolio performance.\n14. Higher demand for policy lending and reduced appetite for investment lending can be traced to a\ncombination of global challenges and domestic policy shifts. Multiple global crises made fast-disbursing DPF\noperations the instrument of choice. Domestically, policy priorities were focused on a few large public\ninvestment projects, limiting space in the federal budget for other CPF focus areas. The government also\nwithdrew earlier requests for World Bank support in several areas, indicating shifting priorities or views of\nthe benefits of external project financing. Changing policy directions, institutional challenges, high turnover\nin key institutions, and security obstacles further affected policy continuity, implementation, and results.\nWith the 2024 electoral transition approaching, the window of opportunity for new policy reforms and the\npreparation of any further IPF pipeline is now limited.29\n15. The World Bank showed flexibility and pragmatism to deliver results through two main\nengagement strategies. First, IBRD responded to changing policy priorities by focusing on policy lending\nduring COVID-19 while continuing to invest in selected shared priorities: forestry preservation, financial\ninclusion, an improved business environment, and gender equality. IFC introduced upstream pre-investment\nprograms to support market creation and project structuring from an early stage, complemented by advisory\nprojects on climate, gender, and digitalization. Second, at client request, several investment operations were\nrestructured by the Bank, with cancellations of US$894 million out of US$1.28 billion in net commitments.\n16. The World Bank has maintained a strong analytical program to inform DPFs, to add value where\nmore intensive financial engagement was not possible, and to explore new entry-points. Demand-driven\nAdvisory Services and Analytics (ASA) informed key issues across the CPF objectives and maintained World\nBank engagement in many areas in the absence of lending (e.g., education, labor market policy, climate\nchange). The Reimbursable Advisory Services (RAS) instrument – a powerful instrument for middle-income\ncountries like Mexico – has been deployed in the transport sector, with a new agreement signed in July 2023.\n17. The CPF’s intended subnational focus on Mexico’s southern states was advanced through policy\nlending and advisory services. The CPF aimed for a greater impact on Mexico’s southern states, where a\nlarge share of the poorest population lives. This objective was affected by the reduced IPF program to\nchannel further resources to the poorest states in the south, as legal and institutional obstacles prevent direct\nlending to subnational government in Mexico. Still, IBRD supported federal policies (through policy-based\nlending) that generated benefits for the whole country, but had positive effects for lagging states (e.g., the\nUrban Upgrading Program supported under the Environmental Sustainability and Urban Resilience DPF\ntargeted marginalized urban areas with above-average deficits in access to public services and infrastructure\nin southern states). Analytical and advisory engagements have allowed demand-driven support to states, for\nexample through just-in-time support in several sectors (e.g., productivity, education, disaster risk\nmanagement), helping identify new partners and pave the way for potential future engagements (Annex 5).\n18. Fiduciary management has been satisfactory. Financial management and procurement\narrangements for IBRD projects are adequate. IBRD has strengthened client procurement capacity and\n29 The choice of lending instrument also relates to the characteristics of the investment project financing (IPF) instrument, particularly\nits disbursement profile, associated commitment fees and implementation modalities.\n4\n\nharmonized procurement documents are being updated.30 IBRD has also supported higher quality financial\nmanagement at national and project levels, in coordination with the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)\nand the national organized accounting profession. Audits are generally on time and unqualified. More\ncustomized, agile approaches, including greater use of digital technologies, will be explored.\n19. The portfolio complied with safeguard policies and the Environmental and Social Framework (ESF).\nProject environmental and social risk ratings have ranged from moderate to low. Targeted supervision and\ncapacity-strengthening for all IPF projects have advanced ESF implementation, including in good practices\nsuch as the Dedicated Grant Mechanism for Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Project.\n20. IFC’s committed portfolio in Mexico stands at US$2.4 billion, leveraging further private-sector\nfinance. As of December 31st, 2023, IFC portfolio included US$827 million of mobilized resources, the fourth\nlargest portfolio regionally and 13th globally. From FY20 to February 2024, IFC has committed US$2.3 billion\nof long-term financing (including US$900 million of mobilized resources) and US$706 million of short-term\nfinancing.\n21. MIGA’s guarantees in Mexico stand at US$2.3 billion, de-risking foreign investments and lending,\nwhich could lead the way to structure more operations with the financial sector. Mexico is MIGA’s largest\nexposure in the LAC region and globally. As of end-FY23, MIGA’s exposure in Mexico was in support of one\nproject in the energy sector. During the CPF, MIGA has provided guarantees to the Federal Electricity\nCommission to rehabilitate and modernize hydropower plants and to the export credit agency Bancomext to\nhelp exporting firms mitigate the impacts of the pandemic, the latter cancelled in April 2023. In October 2023,\nMIGA issued capital optimization guarantees of $1.9 billion to HSBC, covering the risk of expropriation of\nfunds for the mandatory reserves that HSBC Mexico holds at the central bank of Mexico.\nProgress Towards CPF Objectives\n22. Despite progress, many CPF targets are not on track. The global and domestic shifts described above\nhave both affected CPF implementation and results. Of 24 objective indicators, (i) eight have been achieved,\n(ii) one is partially achieved, (iii) five are on track but require adjustments to be achieved, and (iv) ten are off\ntrack, five of which are unlikely to be achieved as a result of cancellations or restructurings.31 In some areas,\nprogress has been made through other channels, e.g., DPF or ASA as opposed to IPF (see Annexes 3 and 5).\nFocus Area A: Supporting more rapid, more inclusive growth.\n23. In Focus Area A, of six indicators, one is achieved, one is partially achieved, one is on track, and\nthree are off track. Challenges are mainly due to cancellations and restructurings of operations, pandemic\nimpact, and monetary tightening to contain inflation.\n24. Financial intermediation and inclusion results are mixed. Progress on expanding access to credit for\nMSMEs was constrained by the pandemic, as well as liquidity and solvency issues faced by non-banking\nfinancial institutions (Indicator 1.2: MSMEs loans extended). Additional IBRD financing of the “Expanding\nRural Finance Project” was ruled out by debt ceilings during the pandemic and a shift in policy and lending\npriorities (Indicator 1.1: rural microenterprises and SMEs with access to credit). Financial inclusion has been\nexpanded through electronic payments via the new electronic payment platform (Indicator 1.3).\n25. IBRD has supported subnational competition regulation in the telecom, transport, and retail\nsectors. Forty-one subnational laws and regulations were changed to introduce a more competitive\nregulatory framework, achieving Indicator 2.1 (pro-competitive regulatory framework at the subnational\n30 Seven IPFs, including four trust funds are implementing the procurement regulations, taking into account the benefits of their\nflexibilities according to the context, risk, value, and complexity of each project, supported by its own project procurement strategy.\n31 Achieved: the targets were met on time or ahead of time; Partially achieved: half of the targets or less were met and the indicator\nis dropped in the PLR; On track: more than half of the targets have been met; Off track: half of the targets or less have been met.\n5\n\nlevel). IBRD also supported the conceptualization and design of the national digital platform aimed at\nenhancing business regulation across the regulatory governance cycle. IFC support to Altan Redes (the\ncompany responsible for the shared network) contributed to an increase in the population covered by\nwireless broadband to 70 percent as of January 2022 (Indicator 2.2). Limited progress was observed on\nagriculture storage infrastructure and market price information systems (Indicator 2.3), as a result of the\nJanuary 2020 cancellation of the “Grain Storage and Information for Agricultural Competitiveness Project” at\nclient request, reflecting changes in priorities. A strong ASA program on inclusive growth included the 2022\nProductivity Report and joint IBRD-IFC work on nearshoring, entrepreneurship, and eco-industrial parks.\nFocus Area B: Strengthening institutions for public finance, service delivery, and economic inclusion.\n26. In Focus Area B, of nine indicators, three have been achieved, one is on track, and five are off track.\nOn public service delivery, under Objective 3 (enhance the management of public resources), no progress\nwas made on Indicator 3.1 (population captured by the single identification system) due to the client decision\nto cancel the “National Digital Identity System Project” in 2022, reflecting priority changes. Support to public\nfinance through the “Modernization of Public Financial Management Systems in Mexico Project” was\nconstrained by the pandemic and implementation delays, which limited progress on Indicators 3.2 (budget\nexecuted through the Integrated Financial Management Information System) and 3.3 (time to produce\nconsolidated budget execution information). The project was restructured and is now in problem project\nstatus. IFC’s support to Public Private Partnership (PPP) transactions included two projects successfully\nawarded in May 2021 under the Los Cabos Water Advisory Project (Indicator 3.4: PPP transactions).\n27. Despite the impact of the pandemic, specific results indicators in education and social protection\nservice delivery are encouraging. Objective 4 (strengthen institutional capacity to deliver inclusive social\nservices) has been supported through the national early childhood development strategy (indicator 4.1\nadoption at the state level). Indicator 4.2 (pilots of the new integrated health networks at the state level) was\nnot achieved due to changes in government priorities. IBRD technical assistance was provided to the National\nInstitute of Public Health and the Secretariat of Finance and Public Credit (SHCP). Almost 52,000 additional\npatients were attended between 2018 and 2022 by IFC supported healthcare services, exceeding the targets\nfor Indicator 4.3 (patients benefiting from the healthcare services). Objective 5 (strengthen the capacity of\nthe social protection system for economic inclusion) was achieved through the “Social Protection System\nProject” and support to Mexico’s National Employment Service (SNE). Indicator 5.1 (Integrated Social\nProtection Centers established) was surpassed. Indicator 5.2 (implementation of modernized processes at\nSNE) has also been achieved through support to SNE, including the expansion of its office network.\nFocus Area C: Enabling sustainable infrastructure and climate action.\n28. In Focus Area C four of the nine objective indicators have been achieved; three are on track (after\nsome adjustments); and two are off track. Notable results have been achieved on housing and sustainable\nlandscape management, despite some implementation delays and restructuring.\n29. Progress has been lower than expected under Objective 6 on more inclusive and sustainable\ninfrastructure services. The “Water Security and Resilience of the Valley of Mexico Project” (PROSEGHIR),\napproved in February 2020, was delayed for over two years, slowing progress on Indicator 6.2 (people with\nimproved water supply reliability), but remains a priority in line with the Evolution Roadmap’s global\nchallenge on water security and access. A project to support Mexico’s National Infrastructure Fund\n(FONADIN) was dropped during preparation, affecting Indicator 6.1 (private capital mobilization for\ninfrastructure). Nevertheless, almost US$570 million have been mobilized by the issuance of sustainable\nbonds with a gender perspective by the infrastructure development bank, BANOBRAS. Despite US$800\nmillion of IFC additional private finance mobilization and own account investments in infrastructure, results\nare below expectations for indicator 6.1. MIGA’s guarantee played a catalytic role in the Tierra Mojada Power\nPlant project, which was cancelled in December 2020 owing to the successful refinancing of the project’s\n6\n\nbank debt with a bond, issued after the project had become operational. Although minimum power was\ndelivered during MIGA’s contract period, the project has generated 5,828 GWh of electricity above the target\nfor Indicator 6.4 (total power generated). In June 2023, MIGA also signed a guarantee supporting CFE for the\nrehabilitation and modernization of hydropower plants. The “Energy Efficiency in Public Facilities Project”\n(PRESEMEH) was scaled down to one third of the original US$150 million, reducing progress on Indicators 6.3\n(energy savings) and 7.1 (GHG emissions reduced). Indicator 6.5 (access to low-cost resilient housing) was\nreached through the “Improving Access to Affordable Housing Project” and IFC support to a housing provider.\n30. Progress on Objective 7 on climate change mitigation has been satisfactory. Sustainable landscape\nmanagement practices (Indicator 7.2) and the number of beneficiaries, particularly women and indigenous\npeoples, implementing sustainable management schemes (Indicator 7.3) have increased with satisfactory\nimplementation of the “Strengthening Entrepreneurship in Productive Forest Landscapes Project”\n(PROFOEM). Similarly, power generated from renewable energy sources supported by IFC has achieved its\ntarget (Indicator 7.4), with potential for further support. Indicators 7.1 (GHG emissions) and 7.3 (sustainable\nmanagement schemes in rural areas) were affected by restructurings of the forest, sustainable landscapes,\nand energy projects, but remain a priority to help tackle the global challenge of climate change.\nProgress Towards Corporate Commitments\n31. Corporate commitments have been mainstreamed across World Bank operations. The portfolio has\nexceeded corporate and regional targets since FY21 with 100 percent of IBRD projects gender-tagged and all\nactive projects complying with citizen engagement requirements. On climate, IBRD reached an average of\n41.3 percent of co-benefits in FY21-23, and IFC and MIGA had an average of 54.5 and 44.0 percent\nrespectively of new projects and guarantees tagged as climate-related since FY20 (Annex 6).\nPartnerships and Leveraging\n32. The World Bank has deepened coordination with other development partners. Under the\n“Environment Sustainability and Urban Resilience DPF” prior actions on climate, forests, and environmental\nmanagement were coordinated with partners (Annex 5). The Partnership for Market Implementation, a\nmulti-donor initiative led by the World Bank,32 has provided technical assistance on carbon pricing. The\nUnited Kingdom, the United States, and IBRD have joined forces to support Mexico’s forestry program\n(Objective 7). For the remainder of the CPF, closer collaboration within the WBG and with the IDB is expected,\nincluding on selected global challenges and private capital mobilization.\nIV. EMERGING LESSONS\n33. CPF design needs to adjust to the policy framework and operating environment, taking a\npragmatic, flexible, and creative approach to opportunities and risks. A realistic assessment of country\ncontext – policy priorities, financing constraints, political economy, and implementation capacity and\nlikelihood – is crucial. While the pandemic was impossible to predict, other governance, institutional, and\nsectoral risks could have been assessed more realistically and the program designed accordingly. The PLR\nbuilds on these lessons, with greater program selectivity and a more realistic risk assessment, seeking\nopportunities and balancing what is desirable with what is politically and technically possible at the federal,\nsubnational and sector levels. The Evolution Roadmap, with an enhanced value proposition for middle-\nincome countries, opens opportunities for a more integrated one World Bank Group approach at the project\nlevel and in joint knowledge engagements, such as on nearshoring, climate finance, financial inclusion, digital,\nenergy supply reliability and water. Global knowledge initiatives (e.g., the World Development Report on\nmigration) under the new Knowledge Compact may open doors to new forms of collaboration.\n34. Policy-lending can provide rapid support in response to crises, helping finance public expenditures\n32 Among them the European Commission, Germany, Spain, Sweden, Japan, United Kingdom, United States, and Australia.\n7\n\nand laying the foundations for a resilient recovery, while fostering innovations. In recent decades, Mexico\nhas followed a pattern of countercyclical lending, increasing exposure in downturns and crises (e.g., new\ncommitments of US$10.5 billion in FY08-10 and US$3.6 billion FY20-22). During COVID-19, three of four DPFs\napproved in FY20-22 directly supported pandemic crisis response and recovery, including the first “Build Back\nBetter” DPF. DPFs also supported innovative policy reforms (e.g., fintech, gender, and sustainable finance).\n35. IPF preparation can be challenging and fragile, yet investment lending operations have usually led\nto positive outcomes when implemented. Despite an investment pipeline reflecting the strength of the\ndialogue in many sectors, the government made a series of decisions not to proceed with many IPF\noperations. Complex project design, including inter- and intra-institutional coordination challenges,\nimplementation delays, partial cancellations, have jeopardized impact. Of the five IPF projects that closed in\nFY20-23, three were found satisfactory by the IEG (e.g., simple project design and client ownership\ncontributed to successful implementation). Four additional IPF projects are rated moderately satisfactory or\nbetter and are expected to achieve their objectives by closing in FY25. Reduced appetite for IPF and\nrestrictions on the use of this and other instruments (e.g., PforR) call for a close dialogue on design features,\nimplementation challenges, lessons learned to develop a new federal and potential also subnational pipeline.\n36. Analytical work has added technical value to policy making and served as a technical foundation\nfor policy lending. In the absence of new investment lending, the ASA program has positioned the World\nBank as a trusted adviser on evidence-based policy reforms, strengthening DPF design. Expansion of financial\naccess in Mexico was supported through the Financial Inclusion DPF, the COVID-19 Financial Access DPF, and\nthe Promoting Women’s Economic Opportunities and Sustainable Productivity Growth DPF. These operations\nbuilt on the 2016 and 2022 Financial Sector Assessment Programs, the Financial Inclusion Global Initiative,\nthe Gender Report, and IFC support on business regulations and SME access to credit.\n37. For IFC, deepening the relationship with the Ministry of Finance, diversifying business\ndevelopment efforts across industries and client types, while also emphasizing non-financial benefits, is\nexpected to both mitigate sector concentration risk and strengthen IFC’s value proposition. IFC investments\nin renewable energy in Mexico have grown substantially, representing almost 100 percent of new IFC\ninfrastructure investments from FY18 to FY20. Subsequent difficulties in the sector created a shock to IFC’s\ninfrastructure target, which was only partially mitigated by increased support for digital infrastructure. IFC’s\ndiversification to new players within the financial system and its use of the highest environmental, social, and\ngovernance standards have enhanced IFC’s contribution to the financial inclusion and climate agenda,\nincluded under CPF focus areas A and C. The latter can be illustrated by IFC investments in the Sustainable\nBond issued by GF Mega, structured under a sustainability framework to promote financial inclusion,\nwomen’s empowerment, and climate-friendly practices; and the Partial Credit Guarantee to IDEI Green Bond\nIssuance, to finance Excellence in Design for Greater Efficiencies (EDGE)-certified green residential projects.\nSynergies and complementarities among IFC, IBRD, and MIGA can also strengthen product and market\ndiversification.\nV. ADJUSTMENTS TO COUNTRY PARTNERSHIP FRAMEWORK\n38. This PLR confirms the strategic direction and the duration of the CPF but proposes adjustments to\nthe results framework. The PLR confirms that the three CPF Focus Areas remain appropriate to address\nMexico’s structural growth, inclusion, and sustainability challenges identified in the Systematic Country\nDiagnostic.33 The PLR adjusts Objective 2 to reflect new engagements with the private sector and drops\nObjective 3 to reflect implementation delays and cancellations. Objective 2 includes new indicators on the\nadoption of a framework to certify industrial parks. Focus Area 3 will continue supporting Mexico in some of\nthe most pressing global challenges, e.g., Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation; Energy Access; and\nWater Security and Access. The results matrix has been streamlined to reflect reform space, client demand,\n33 World Bank, “Mexico Systematic Country Diagnostic,” August 2019.\n8\n\nand implementation capacity by sector34 (Annex 2).\nFocus Area A: Supporting more rapid, more inclusive growth.\n39. The PLR maintains Objective 1 to “foster financial intermediation and inclusion” and continues\nsupporting sustainable finance. With limited space for new lending, the PLR proposes to support Mexico’s\nsustainable finance agenda through analytical support, including the development of a biodiversity taxonomy\nand associated regulations, building on the joint IFC-IBRD 30x30 Zero advisory services, which also aims to\nraise the share of green assets in bank portfolios. On financial intermediation, new objectives have been\nadded on increasing account ownership and reducing the gender gap in digital payments. Indicators on users\nand payments through the digital payments’ platform and MSME loans have been revised to reflect progress.\n40. Objective 2 is adjusted from aiming to “reduce regulatory and competition barriers to economic\ngrowth” to “support private sector investment.” IBRD will support ASA on the subnational implementation\nof a national digital platform to improve business regulation. Based on analytical work on productivity,\nentrepreneurship, and eco-industrial parks, IBRD and IFC will support FDI attraction for the piloting of eco-\nindustrial parks. The PLR includes two new indicators on a framework to certify industrial parks including\nenvironmental sustainability and the number of IFC transactions tagged as “nearshoring.”\nFocus Area B: Strengthening institutions for service delivery and inclusion.\n41. Focus Area B is adjusted from “Strengthening institutions for public finance, service delivery, and\neconomic inclusion” to “Strengthening institutions for service delivery and inclusion.” The PLR drops\nObjective 3 to “enhance the management of public resources” due to project implementation delays and\ncancellations. IBRD will continue supporting SHCP plans to strengthen human resource and public investment\nmanagement and its integration with the budget system as well as the implementation of Mexico’s\nsustainable finance strategy. IFC will continue structuring and financing PPP projects in key sectors, yet with\nmore limited scope, given reduced federal funding to structure projects and uneven subnational capacities.\n42. Objective 4 (new Objective 3) is revised to “strengthen the capacity to deliver inclusive education\nand health services.” IBRD will remain engaged in early childhood education and support the recovery of\nlearning losses by (i) improving teaching practices; (ii) developing early warning systems to identify students\nat risk of dropping out and targeted efforts to lower this risk; and (iii) catalyzing human capital formation by\nexpanding early childhood care and education services. IFC will continue to support innovative, scalable\nmodels for high-quality, affordable private health-care services. In line with the pandemic prevention and\npreparedness global challenge, IBRD has shifted its engagement to formulating proposals to enhance\nreadiness and responses for health crises and the resilience of health financing to population ageing.\n43. Objective 5 (new Objective 4) is adjusted to “strengthen the capacity to deliver inclusive social\nservices.” IBRD work with SNE to use good practices and digital tools to support social protection and\nimprove the integration of vulnerable members of the labor force (e.g., women, youth, and migrants).\nFocus Area C: Enabling sustainable infrastructure and climate action.\n44. Objective 6 (new Objective 5) on more inclusive and sustainable infrastructure services remains a\npriority, despite challenges in infrastructure sectors. In the water sector, IBRD will continue to support water\nsupply and sanitation activities through its operation in the Valley of Mexico and potential investments and\nadvisory work in the northern part of the country (e.g., wastewater treatment and reuse, groundwater\nmonitoring and management, and irrigation modernization) in line with the water security and access global\nchallenge. The energy efficiency project (PRESEMEH) will be closed. MIGA will expand its presence through\nits guarantee to CFE supporting efforts towards tackling the global challenge on energy efficiency and access.\nIBRD will support Mexico’s development banks on private sector capital mobilization, especially for\n34 The PLR drops nine, adjusts thirteen, and reconfirms two out of 24 objective indicators, and adds five new indicators.\n9\n\ninfrastructure and energy. IFC will support competitive private energy markets and digital infrastructure,\nfocusing on renewables and telecom, in addition to other areas where private investment can help meeting\nthe growing needs from nearshoring. WBG collaboration with financial entities and funds will be\nstrengthened to promote private capital mobilization to address infrastructure needs in key sectors such as\ntransport, energy, water, and real estate. Indicators have been adjusted to reflect progress and challenges.\n45. The World Bank will intensify efforts to support Mexico’s climate change goals under Objective 7\n(new Objective 6). Cross-sectoral climate change mitigation and adaptation, one of the key global challenges\noutlined in the Evolution Roadmap, will be supported through ASA. IFC will mobilize knowledge and\ninvestments targeted at financial institutions, fintech, and funds to develop sound climate portfolios. MIGA\nwill seek opportunities to support renewable energy projects. MIGA has also issued new capital optimization\nguarantees to HSBC covering the risk of expropriation of funds for the mandatory reserves that HSBC Mexico\nholds at the central bank of Mexico. The benefits of the MIGA-enabled capital relief will provide financing of\nUS$1.5 billion to climate finance sub-projects over 2023-2026. IBRD operations on forestry and agriculture\nwill continue with a focus on forest-dependent communities and on strengthening community and producer\norganizations in the management and entrepreneurship of productive landscapes, protecting biodiversity\nand nature as a key global challenge. The World Bank will seek innovation in financial solutions for disaster\nmanagement and climate resilience and support the sustainable taxonomy. Objectives have been adjusted.\nVI. RISKS TO THE CPF PROGRAM\n46. The overall risk for this CPF has increased from Moderate to Substantial. Table shows the risk\nassessment, using the Systematic Operations Risk-rating Tool (SORT). The World Bank will carefully assess\nand mitigate risks through the electoral transition to build a close engagement with the new administration.\nTable 1. SORT: Risks to the CPF program\nRisk categories CPF Rating PLR Rating\n47. Political and Governance Risk is\nPolitical and governance M S\nincreased from moderate to substantial.\nMacroeconomic M M\nSectoral strategies and policies S S Program implementation could be delayed\nTechnical design of projects or programs M M ahead of the presidential election in June\nInstitutional capacity for implementation 2024 and the following transition period,\nM S\nand sustainability\nincluding restrictions on new reforms. To\nFiduciary M M\nmitigate this risk, IBRD will focus on: (i)\nEnvironment and social M M\naccelerating portfolio implementation and\nStakeholders M M\nOther (crime and violence) S S addressing project risks; (ii) preparing a\nOverall M S transition engagement strategy (including\nNote: Summary risks: H = high; S = substantial; M = moderate; L = low transition notes) and rapidly engaging with\nthe new administration; and (iii) seeking opportunities for subnational dialogue.\n48. Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability risk has risen to substantial.\nImplementation challenges have increased, affecting planning, management, budgeting, and monitoring and\nevaluation. Turnover of government staff, limited experience with international financial institutions,\nprocess-intensive implementation, regulatory and bureaucratic hurdles, and procurement delays have\nincreased transaction times, requiring the restructuring of implementation arrangements in several projects.\nTo mitigate these risks, IBRD has strengthened the coordination with government, created early warning\nmechanisms across the portfolio, and enhanced implementation readiness on new investment projects.\n49. Insecurity, crime, and violence continue to pose substantial risks to CPF implementation. Security\nchallenges affect beneficiaries and staff, impede mission travel and delay operations. To mitigate the risks,\nengagements in high-risk areas are limited, remote monitoring is used, security is closely monitored, and\nsecurity training and support is provided in line with the Mexico Office Security Strategy.\n10", "excerpt_sha256": "b763926bac1a6c5b30325aa396d5673b862ca81e59b2591b1cc7ac00c8b67616", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt directly supports the indicator counts, pandemic and institutional factors, client-requested restructurings/cancellations, and continued policy lending and analytical support."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0052-E020", "source_id": "SRC-240044c0f4fd6c00", "url": "https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099032124080524844/pdf/BOSIB15b3ebe810f518e201c5cfce8f25cd.pdf", "title": "Mexico Social Protection Public Expenditure and Institutional Review", "publisher": "World Bank", "raw_sha256": "5af01216a7eaa150c1c22653f14028213e569bb7de3bea266525c487ae7bc343", "fact_summary": "During FY2020-2023, the World Bank recorded a sharp shift toward policy lending, ten project closures, four partial cancellations, four projects dropped during preparation, and two unsigned loan offers. Program Implementation\nProgram and Portfolio Performance\n1. World Bank lending to Mexico has increased. As of December 2023, the World Bank active\nportfolio includes US$1.68 billion from the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development\n(IBRD), US$2.4 billion committed portfolio from the International Finance Corporation (IFC), and US$536\nmillion from the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA). Under the current CPF, the total\nIBRD lending approved sums up to US$4.3 billion (US$1.1 billion average per year) relative to US$560.0\nmillion in the previous CPF. The International Finance Corporation (IFC) also committed investments\nincreased in the first four years relative to the same period of the previous CPF, from US$1.62 billion\nduring FY14-17 to US$1.75 billion during FY20-23. Responding to global context and increased client\nappetite for countercyclical financing sources, DPF increased to US$3.9 billion compared to US$0.5 billion\nunder the previous CPF.228 Over FY20-FY23, four Development Policy Financing (DPF) operations were\nextended to the Mexican authorities, three directly supporting the COVID-19 crisis response and\nrecovery, including IBRD’s first Build Back Better DPF. For FY24, the pipeline for the remaining CPF\nconsists of only one DPF project. In addition, IBRD has an active Bank-executed trust fund portfolio\nbenefiting the country which comprises 34 trust funds for a total net commitment of US$71.1 million.\n2. IBRD portfolio has moved from an average of sixteen projects during the previous CPF to ten\nprojects during the current CPF, with a gradual reduction of investment project financing (IPF). IPF was\nreduced from 11 projects in 2020 to 7 in 2023. The active IBRD portfolio as of December 2023, comprises\nfive IBRD loans (US$1.6 billion), complemented by two Global Environment Facility (GEF) projects, and\none recipient executed trust-funded project. Since the CPF was discussed by the Board, seven IBRD\nprojects and one GEF stand-alone project have been approved. During this same period, ten projects\nclosed, four operations were partially cancelled, four were prepared but dropped at later stages of\npreparation, and two were not signed resulting in withdrawn loan offers (Table 5.2).\nTable 5.1. Restructurings have been a key component of portfolio management, including: (i) partial\ncancellations (e.g., US$100 million of the Energy Efficiency in Public Facilities Project and US$45 million of\nthe Modernization of Public Financial Management Systems in Mexico Project); (ii) extensions of closing\ndates (including in half of the total restructurings to date – eleven extensions from FY20-23); (iii)\nadjustment to the Results Framework as well as changes to components and costs, are some of the\nproactive actions taken to address implementation bottlenecks.\n66\n\nTable 5.4. IBRD portfolio trends, FY19-23, including Recipient Executed Trust Funds\nFY FY19 FY20 FY21 FY22 FY23\nPORTFOLIO AND DISBURSEMENTS\nNumber of Projects Approved 2 3 3 2 1\nFinancing Approved (US$M) 900.0 1230.0 1725.0 713.8 700.0\nAverage Financing per Project (US$M) 450.0 410.0 575.0 356.9 700.0\nNumber of Active Projects 11 12 10 9 9\nNet Commitments (US$M) 2,481.5 3,091.5 2,194.7 1,308.4 1,738.4\nDisbursement in FY (US$ M) (Active Portfolio) 394.1 41.6 48.0 17.9 737.5\nTotal Undisbursed Balance (US$M) 1,448.1 2014.4 2039.1 1228.6 919.4\nDisbursement Ratio (%, IPF only) 43.3 4.5 10.2 4.4 7.4\nPORTFOLIO RISKINESS\nProblem Projects (#) 1 1 1 1 1\nProblem Projects (%) 9.1 8.3 10.0 11.1 11.1\nPotential Problem Project # 0 0 0 0 0\nProjects at Risk (#) 1 1 1 1 1\nProjects at Risk (%) 9.1 8.3 10.0 11.1 11.1\nCommitments at Risk (US$M) 55.0 21.9 21.9 21.9 65.0\nCommitments at Risk (%) 2.2 0.7 1.0 1.7 3.7\n12 month rolling Proactivity (%) 66.7 100.0 0.0 0.0 100.0\nSource. World Bank Business Intelligence Reporting, Standard Reports, and Analysis for Office.\n4. A strong analytical program helped maintain the dialogue and explore entry-points for further\ncollaboration with the current and the next administration. Formal Advisory Services and Analytics (ASA),\nsuch as the Female Labor Force Participation Report,229 the Pensions Policy Support Program, and the\nProductivity Report,230 helped to set up the agenda, informing reform options and lending, such as in the\n“Promoting Women Economic Opportunities and Sustainable Productivity Growth DPF” operation. Just-\nin-time support, such as for the education, health, social protection and labor sectors, helped maintain\nthe dialogue and pave the ground for future engagements (e.g., an assessment of learning lost during\nschool closures). IBRD has become a key partner in assisting the authorities implement a comprehensive\nearly childhood education and development strategy; IBRD’s analytical support has been welcomed by\nthe Secretariat of Finance and Public Credit (SHCP) and health sector agencies to assess the sustainability\nand resilience of health financing in the face of shocks and population ageing; while Mexico has turned\nrepeatedly to IBRD’s Social Protection and Jobs practice for support on pension reform, modernizing its\nlabor market intermediation services and for support in integrating migrants into the economy. In\naddition, IBRD’s engagement focused strongly on helping the government advance the climate resilience,\nforestry, and land use agendas. As a result, in 2020 an innovative “Environmental Sustainability and Urban\nResilience DPF” was approved, which addressed development challenges around the increase of the\nresilience of the most vulnerable municipalities, the gradual reform towards non-deforestation criteria in\nagriculture support programs, and local air quality provisions to mitigate black carbon. Likewise, this DPF\nalso strongly featured IBRD’s urban engagement. Strong operational engagement in the water sector has\nbeen further supported by analytical work laying the foundation for strengthening IBRD’s engagement in\nthe sector at national and subnational levels. Engagements with the SHCP on disaster risk management,\ne.g., technical assistance to strengthen the risk management strategy related to natural hazard risk at the\nfederal and subnational levels, helped respond to core client demands and laid the foundation for longer-\nterm reforms on the Evolution Roadmap global challenges’ agenda. ASAs also led to demand in trust-funds\nand other concessional funding.\n5. Reimbursable Advisory Services are a powerful engagement instrument for middle-income\ncountries. A Reimbursable Advisory Services (RAS) agreement was signed in transport in June 2023 and\n229 World Bank, “Female Labor Force Participation in Mexico,” March 2021.\n230 World Bank, “Productivity Growth in Mexico: Understanding Main Dynamics and Key Drivers,” January 2022.\n67\n\nother RAS engagements were explored in line with sector demand in the education and health sectors but\ndid not materialize.\n6. Given legal challenges for direct lending to the sub-national level and security risks, IBRD relied\non ASA to support subnational. For example, IBRD has supported the municipalities of Tulum and San\nCristobal de las Casas in developing their Municipal Climate Action Plans, and in March 2023, launched a\ntechnical assistance program (financed by the Climate Investment Facility) to develop a city climate action\nplan for the metropolitan area of Oaxaca, including all the 25 municipalities, located in one of the\ncountry’s poorest states in the south.\n7. Similarly, advisory programs helped to keep a strong involvement from public and private\ninstitutions in priority areas such as climate. Under the 30x30 Zero project in Mexico, for instance,\nIFC/IBRD collaborated with Mexico’s Central Bank (Banxico), originally through the Sustainable Finance\nCommittee, part of the Financial Stability Board, to examine the different aspects of “greening the\nfinancial sector”: (i) Green Taxonomy, (ii) reporting and disclosure, (iii) social and climate risk analysis, and\n(iv) ecoefficiency and decarbonization. A gender agenda with financial institutions and regulators is being\ncarried out jointly by IFC and IBRD to address policy and market challenges to untap and scale new\nopportunities. IFC participated in the four working groups and supported the green Taxonomy, which\nbecame the Sustainable Taxonomy. Also, through the Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) Green\nBuildings program, IFC has supported the public and private sectors’ green agenda, reaching 4.9 million\nsquare meters certified with the Excellence in Design for Greater Efficiencies (EDGE) certificate, and\nthrough the TechEmerge Sustainable Cooling Cities program, the successful closure of 11 pilots by FY23\nwhere participants were able to decide on potential scale-up of innovate cooling solutions either at\nadopter or at broader market. This EDGE achievement positions Mexico as the third country globally with\nthe largest certified floor space. IBRD and IFC are also working together on how to harness nearshoring\ninvestments in Mexico for which a close coordination with the government would be key. Teams are\nworking on a strategy to leverage this opportunity to address development gaps (notably declining\nproductivity, high informality, asymmetric integration into global value chains, and low technological\nupgrade by local firms), while promoting a sustainable and inclusive approach of these investments and\npublic-private coordination to provide the enabling factors. MIGA is also planning to join those strategic\nconversation as one World Bank to explore opportunities to deploy MIGA de-risking solutions to enhance\nbankability of projects, facilitate public-private investments, and the mobilization of foreign capital in\nMexico. More specifically, the close collaboration with IBRD and IFC proved crucial in the development of\nthe Federal Commission Electricity (CFE) project, where MIGA provided non-honoring guarantees to a\nstate-owned utility, given rapidly evolving conditions in the energy sector.\"\n8. Portfolio management has incorporated fiduciary considerations. On procurement, tailored\nproject procurement strategies have been designed in line with each project’s context, risks, regulations,\nand complexities, allowing for flexibility and better execution of Procurement Plans, but also to overcome\nchallenges. These include misalignment of the procurement processes to optimize the use of resources in\ncontracts, and lack of government staff with experience conducting procurement processes financed with\nIBRD resources. Mitigation measures have included additional support to implementing entities, including\nin the use of innovative methods such as the use of rated criteria, and harmonization of procurement\ndocuments with the most recent country’s regulations and policies. Performance on financial\nmanagement continues to be generally satisfactory. Challenges related to budgeting and limited fiscal\nspace for investment operations, including trust funds, remain. IBRD loan proceeds are budgeted only on\nan annual basis, and grant funds cannot be received directly by federal entities, affecting planning and\ncausing implementation delays. Limited federal budgetary resources have also led to a reduction in the\nscope of some investment projects. To tackle these challenges, IBRD has continued to promote fiduciary\n68\n\ncapacity development for project-implementing entities through the provision of regular training (i.e., on\nIBRD’s systematic tracking of exchanges in procurement, STEP) and workshops (i.e., a fiduciary workshop\nheld in June 2023), and through actively engaging with the organized accounting profession in Mexico in\ncoordination with the Inter-American Development Bank.\n9. Safeguards ratings and environment and social (E&S) standards performance ranged between\nmoderately satisfactory and satisfactory, with no projects closed with pending E&S measures. IBRD has\nprovided oversight, supervision, follow-up, and capacity-building support, for all IPF projects. One of the\ninnovations of the Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) is the recognition that climate change is\naffecting the nature and location of IBRD-financed projects. It sets out a vision to support client countries\nto decarbonize and invest in resilience to help overcome climate change as a fundamental threat to\ndevelopment in our lifetime. Although the requirement for new operations, the Paris Alignment\nassessment, is distinct from the E&S assessment set out in the ESF, the ESF’s standards reinforce the World\nBank’s ability to ensure financing aligns with the goals of the Paris Agreement.\n10. Complementing IBRD’s lending, IFC’s committed portfolio in Mexico stands at US$2.4 billion as\nof December 2023, helping leverage private sector financing. As of December 31st, 2023, IFC portfolio\nincluded US$827 million of mobilized resources, making this the fourth largest portfolio regionally and the\n13th globally. From FY20 to February 2024, IFC has committed US$2.3 billion of long-term financing\n(including US$900 million of mobilized resources) and US$706 million of short-term financing in Mexico.\nThe main transactions focused on expanding access to health through low-cost generic medicine,\nincreasing financial inclusion by supporting FinTech, insurance, and non-banking financial institutions,\nhelping job recovery in the tourism sector, promoting digital infrastructure by investing in data centers,\nand supporting renewable energy and green buildings.\nTable 5.5. MIGA has also supported private sector development through guarantees. MIGA issued new\nguarantees for the Tierra Mojada project in FY17, achieving a total private capital mobilization of US$716\nmillion over a notional guarantee amount of US$963 million. In December 2020, the guarantee was\ncancelled due to the successful refinancing of the project’s bank debt with capital markets (bond). In 2020\nMIGA signed guarantees for US$675 million to support export-oriented firms under significant liquidity\nstress due to the COVID-19 crisis, providing liquidity to Bancomext, the government’s export credit\nagency. In April 2023, the guarantee was cancelled due to the full pre-payment of the guaranteed loan. In\nJune 2023, MIGA issued a US$536 million guarantee to support the repowering and upgrade works of\nvarious existing hydropower plants of CFE, Mexico's state-owned integrated electric utility. More recently,\nMIGA issued capital optimization guarantees of $1.9 billion to HSBC in October 2023, covering the risk of\n231 Anticipated Impact Measurement and Monitoring.\n69\n\nexpropriation of funds for the mandatory reserves that HSBC Mexico holds at the central bank of Mexico.\nThe benefits of the MIGA enabled-capital relief will be directly used by HSBC toward accelerating climate\nfinance sub-projects over a three-year period.
